========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, Doctor. Well, what brings me in is... I just feel like I’m constantly running on a motor lately. Like, I finish one thing at school—I teach special education, by the way. Middle school. Which is chaotic enough, but I really do love it. Just the other day, I was helping one of my kids, Jason, with this math worksheet, and he's got this intense fascination with trains, so we always have to make the word problems about trains to keep him engaged. I actually took a train down to Chicago last summer, the Amtrak, and the dining car was completely out of coffee by 9 AM. Can you believe that? Who runs a train and runs out of coffee that early? The conductor looked so stressed out. 

But anyway, what I mean is, I finish one thing at school, or even at home, and I just immediately jump right into the next thing without ever being able to shut myself off. Like I'll get home and start grading papers, and then suddenly I'm out in the yard pulling weeds, and then I'm fixing a hinge on the back door. I just can't stop the motor, you know? It's just... go, go, go.

Well, I mean, I've always been an active guy, but this specific level of just... not being able to stop? That really started about eight months ago. That's when our older relative moved in with us. My wife's uncle, actually. We had to completely clear out the downstairs guest room for him, which used to be my office—well, more like a storage room if I'm being honest. 

While I was clearing it out, I actually found this huge box of my old vinyl records! I spent half the night trying to hook up my old turntable, but the drive belt was completely rotted out. You know how rubber gets when it just sits in a dry room for twenty years? It just crumbles right in your hands like dust. I had to order a replacement part from this little specialty audio shop all the way out in Ohio. The guy on the phone there was great, though, he knew exactly what model I was talking about just from the description of the tonearm. 

But, wait, what was I... oh, right. Yeah, the motor feeling. It's been about eight months. Ever since he moved in, it's just been dialed up to an eleven.

No, no, nothing like that. It hasn't been like a rollercoaster or anything, you know? It's just... it's just this one steady, constant hum. Just one speed the whole time. Now you can't even get a gumball for a quarter. I think they want like a dollar for those machines now. It's robbery! And the gumballs are always stale anyway. 

But yeah, the treadmill... I went all the way out there, and the belt was completely warped. Just totally useless. So I didn't buy it. But no, to answer your question, it hasn't been up and down. Just the same constant motor since the uncle moved in.

Well, I can focus sometimes, I guess, but mostly it's... my mind is definitely jumping around. Like with the grading, I'll be looking at a paper—I teach special ed, mostly middle schoolers right now—and I'll see a kid wrote something about, say, a dog. I was at the pet store last week looking for them, and they had this huge display of those little sweaters for dogs. Who puts a sweater on a golden retriever? They have a double coat! It's ridiculous. The girl working there tried to tell me it was for "anxiety," like a thunder shirt, but it just looked like a cable-knit argyle pattern to me.

So, no, I wouldn't say I'm really focusing on the weeds or the fixing or any of it. My brain is just kind of bouncing off the walls the whole time.

I was at the pet store last week looking for them, and they had this huge display of those little sweaters for dogs. Who puts a sweater on a golden retriever? They have a double coat! Oh, no, it's not really worries. I'm not... I'm not sitting there stressing about the mortgage or the state of the world or anything like that. It's really just totally random stuff that pops in. Like, I'll be thinking about whether I need to buy more wood glue for this chair I'm trying to fix.

Well, yeah, it's definitely causing problems. I wish it was just the way I operate, but... at work, the complaints have actually gotten serious enough that the principal put me on a corrective plan. A corrective plan! I haven't been on one of those since my very first year of teaching. Back then, they used those carbon copy forms, you know? You had to press down really hard with a ballpoint pen to make sure the yellow and pink sheets got the ink. I always hated the pink sheets. They smelled weird, like chemicals. Actually, all paper smelled different back then. Mimeograph machines! Do you remember those? The purple ink? I used to love handing out those worksheets, the kids would all sniff the paper before they started their math. You can't do that with laser printers. It's just hot toner. 

But anyway, yeah, the corrective plan. And at home, it's not much better. My spouse says the caregiving routines for my uncle are just so much harder now because everyone has to manage *my* energy on top of everything else. Which isn't fair to her. She's already doing so much, organizing his pills and everything. Those daily pill organizers are so flimsy, by the way. The little plastic lids snap off after like two weeks. I tried supergluing the 'Thursday' lid back on last week, but the glue got stuck to my thumb, and I spent an hour trying to peel it off with nail polish remover. Acetone is terrible for your skin, just dries it right out. I had to use this heavy-duty lotion my wife keeps by the kitchen sink, the one with the oatmeal in it. 

What was I... oh. The distress, right. Yeah, it is distressing. I feel like I’m damaging trust with my colleagues and my family. And the worst part is, I’m really just trying to help! I want to be helpful, but I guess I'm just making everything harder for everyone.

Actually, all paper smelled different back then. Mimeograph machines! Do you remember those? The purple ink? I used to love handing out those worksheets, the kids would all sniff the paper before they started their math. You can't do that with laser printers. It's just hot toner. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, the corrective plan. And at home, it's not much better. My spouse says the caregiving routines for my uncle are just so much harder now because everyone has to manage *my* energy on top of everything else. Which isn't fair to her. She's already doing so much, organizing his pills and everything. Those daily pill organizers are so flimsy, by the way. The little plastic lids snap off after like two weeks. I tried supergluing the 'Thursday' lid back on last week, but the glue got stuck to my thumb, and I spent an hour trying to peel it off with nail polish remover. Acetone is terrible for your skin, just dries it right out. I had to use this heavy-duty lotion my wife keeps by the kitchen sink, the one with the oatmeal in it. \n\nWhat was I... oh. The distress, right. Yeah, it is distressing. I feel like I’m damaging trust with my colleagues and my family. And the worst part is, I’m really just trying to help! I want to be helpful, but I guess I'm just making everything harder for everyone.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you're trying to do something—like grading those papers or fixing something around the house—do you actually have trouble getting started on it, or trouble finishing it once you've started? ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, getting started isn't really the problem. It's exactly what you said at the end there. Actually, that's really what brings me in here in the first place. I just feel like I’m constantly running on a motor lately. Like I finish one thing at school or at home, and I immediately jump right into the next without ever being able to shut myself off. 

Like yesterday, I finished grading a batch of math quizzes—we're doing fractions right now. I tried using pizza slices on the board to explain common denominators this year, but one of my kids is lactose intolerant and got really distracted talking about dairy allergies, which I completely didn't see coming. You really have to be careful with food analogies nowadays. When I was in middle school, teachers just brought in whatever they wanted. I remember my science teacher, Mr. Henderson, bringing in a whole sheet cake once just to show us tectonic plates. 

But anyway, I finish the quizzes, and instantly I'm up and deciding I need to fix the screen on the back porch. But to do that, you need one of those little spline rollers. So I go out to the garage to find the roller, and I see the lawnmower, and I realize I never changed the spark plug. Have you ever tried to find a replacement spark plug for a fifteen-year-old Toro mower? It's practically impossible. I went down this huge internet rabbit hole about small engine repair. There's this guy on YouTube who does these amazing tutorials from his shed in Minnesota. He has this massive beard, kind of like ZZ Top, and he always starts his videos by drinking coffee out of this giant plaid thermos. I used to have a thermos just like it, actually, the old Stanley ones that weigh about ten pounds. They don't make them like that anymore, the new ones dent if you just look at them wrong. 

But... wait, what was I saying? Oh. Getting started. No, I start things just fine. It's just that motor feeling. I just jump to the next thing and the next thing and I can't shut it off.

I haven't really noticed it coming and going like that. It just feels like it's always on. Every single day, from the minute I wake up. Which is usually around 5 AM, because the neighbor's dog—this little terrier mix, cute thing, but man, does he have a set of lungs on him. He starts barking at the garbage trucks. Our neighborhood has this new waste management contract, and they switched the pickup days from Tuesday to Thursday. Let me tell you, it threw the whole street off. People were leaving their bins out for three days straight. The HOA president, Susan, she sent out this passive-aggressive email about \"curb appeal\" and \"community standards.\" She's always on about the community standards. I remember when she tried to get everyone to paint their mailboxes the exact same shade of beige. \"Desert Sand,\" I think it was called. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I haven't really noticed it stopping or anything. It's just... constant. Just always going.\n\nDoctor: During these eight months, have there been times when you felt even MORE energized, more talkative, or needed less sleep than usual—like a few days or a week where things felt especially intense—followed by times when things calmed down a bit? Or has it really just been this same constant level the whole time?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. It hasn't been like a rollercoaster or anything, you know? It's just... it's just this one steady, constant hum. Just one speed the whole time. Like a treadmill that's stuck on a jog. Speaking of treadmills, I actually tried to buy one off Craigslist a few weeks ago to maybe burn off some of this energy. The guy lived out in the suburbs, right near that old mall they just tore down. It's a shame about that mall, honestly. I used to go there when I was a teenager, they had this incredible arcade. Pac-Man, Galaga, Asteroids... I could spend hours in there with just a pocket full of quarters. Quarters used to go so much further back then, didn't they? Now you can't even get a gumball for a quarter. I think they want like a dollar for those machines now. It's robbery! And the gumballs are always stale anyway. \n\nBut yeah, the treadmill... I went all the way out there, and the belt was completely warped. Just totally useless. So I didn't buy it. But no, to answer your question, it hasn't been up and down. Just the same constant motor since the uncle moved in.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing these activities—grading papers, pulling weeds, fixing things around the house—are you able to focus and concentrate on what you're doing, or do you find your mind is jumping around from one thought to another?\n\nPatient: Well, I can focus sometimes, I guess, but mostly it's... my mind is definitely jumping around. Like with the grading, I'll be looking at a paper—I teach special ed, mostly middle schoolers right now—and I'll see a kid wrote something about, say, a dog. And then I'm thinking about my neighbor's dog, the terrier mix I mentioned, and then I'm wondering if I remembered to buy dog treats for when my brother visits, because his golden retriever has this sensitive stomach. You have to get these specific grain-free salmon bites, they cost an absolute fortune. I was at the pet store last week looking for them, and they had this huge display of those little sweaters for dogs. Who puts a sweater on a golden retriever? They have a double coat! It's ridiculous. The girl working there tried to tell me it was for \"anxiety,\" like a thunder shirt, but it just looked like a cable-knit argyle pattern to me. \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, the papers. Yeah, I'll be grading, and then suddenly I'm up and checking the pantry for the salmon treats, and the paper is just sitting there. So, no, I wouldn't say I'm really focusing on the weeds or the fixing or any of it. My brain is just kind of bouncing off the walls the whole time.\n\nDoctor: When your mind is jumping around like that, bouncing from one thought to another—are those thoughts mostly worries or concerns about things that could go wrong, or are they just random thoughts and ideas that pop into your head without any particular worry attached to them?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, it's not really worries. I'm not... I'm not sitting there stressing about the mortgage or the state of the world or anything like that. It's really just totally random stuff that pops in. Like, I'll be thinking about whether I need to buy more wood glue for this chair I'm trying to fix. \n\nI was at Home Depot the other day, actually, looking for wood glue, and I realized they completely rearranged the aisles again. Why do they do that? You finally figure out where the fasteners are, and suddenly they've moved them all the way over near the garden supplies. I spent twenty minutes just looking for a specific size of wingnut. The guy helping me—older guy, name tag said Carl—he told me corporate makes them change the layout every two years just to force people to walk past different products and buy things they don't need. Seems like a terrible business model if you ask me, because I just got frustrated and left without the wingnuts. \n\nI ended up going to this little local hardware store downtown instead. You know, the one with the squeaky wooden floors? They've been there since the 70s. The owner's son runs it now, and he still uses one of those old heavy metal cash registers with the big buttons. The bell on it has this great ring to it, you know? Not like those terrible electronic beeps at the self-checkout. I can't stand the self-checkout. It always tells me there's an unexpected item in the bagging area when there isn't. \n\nBut yeah, what were we... oh. No, not worries. Just random things like that.\n\nDoctor: Has this constant motor feeling and the jumping around of your thoughts been causing you problems—like at work, in your relationships, or just making you feel distressed? Or does it feel more like just the way you operate?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, it's definitely causing problems. I wish it was just the way I operate, but... at work, the complaints have actually gotten serious enough that the principal put me on a corrective plan. A corrective plan! I haven't been on one of those since my very first year of teaching. Back then, they used those carbon copy forms, you know? You had to press down really hard with a ballpoint pen to make sure the yellow and pink sheets got the ink. I always hated the pink sheets. They smelled weird, like chemicals. Actually, all paper smelled different back then. Mimeograph machines! Do you remember those? The purple ink? I used to love handing out those worksheets, the kids would all sniff the paper before they started their math. You can't do that with laser printers. It's just hot toner. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, the corrective plan. And at home, it's not much better. My spouse says the caregiving routines for my uncle are just so much harder now because everyone has to manage *my* energy on top of everything else. Which isn't fair to her. She's already doing so much, organizing his pills and everything. Those daily pill organizers are so flimsy, by the way. The little plastic lids snap off after like two weeks. I tried supergluing the 'Thursday' lid back on last week, but the glue got stuck to my thumb, and I spent an hour trying to peel it off with nail polish remover. Acetone is terrible for your skin, just dries it right out. I had to use this heavy-duty lotion my wife keeps by the kitchen sink, the one with the oatmeal in it. \n\nWhat was I... oh. The distress, right. Yeah, it is distressing. I feel like I’m damaging trust with my colleagues and my family. And the worst part is, I’m really just trying to help! I want to be helpful, but I guess I'm just making everything harder for everyone.\n\nDoctor: When you're trying to do something—like grading those papers or fixing something around the house—do you actually have trouble getting started on it, or trouble finishing it once you've started? Or is it more that you can do these things, but you just feel this constant urge to move on to the next thing?\n\nPatient: Well, getting started isn't really the problem. It's exactly what you said at the end there. Actually, that's really what brings me in here in the first place. I just feel like I’m constantly running on a motor lately. Like I finish one thing at school or at home, and I immediately jump right into the next without ever being able to shut myself off. \n\nLike yesterday, I finished grading a batch of math quizzes—we're doing fractions right now. I tried using pizza slices on the board to explain common denominators this year, but one of my kids is lactose intolerant and got really distracted talking about dairy allergies, which I completely didn't see coming. You really have to be careful with food analogies nowadays. When I was in middle school, teachers just brought in whatever they wanted. I remember my science teacher, Mr. Henderson, bringing in a whole sheet cake once just to show us tectonic plates. \n\nBut anyway, I finish the quizzes, and instantly I'm up and deciding I need to fix the screen on the back porch. But to do that, you need one of those little spline rollers. So I go out to the garage to find the roller, and I see the lawnmower, and I realize I never changed the spark plug. Have you ever tried to find a replacement spark plug for a fifteen-year-old Toro mower? It's practically impossible. I went down this huge internet rabbit hole about small engine repair. There's this guy on YouTube who does these amazing tutorials from his shed in Minnesota. He has this massive beard, kind of like ZZ Top, and he always starts his videos by drinking coffee out of this giant plaid thermos. I used to have a thermos just like it, actually, the old Stanley ones that weigh about ten pounds. They don't make them like that anymore, the new ones dent if you just look at them wrong. \n\nBut... wait, what was I saying? Oh. Getting started. No, I start things just fine. It's just that motor feeling. I just jump to the next thing and the next thing and I can't shut it off.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I'm not really sure, to be honest. I haven't really noticed if I'm actively trying to stop myself or not. I don't think I even realize I'm doing it until I'm already halfway through the next thing, you know? Like... there's no debate in my head about staying focused. It's just, boom, I'm doing something else. 

Like the other night, I was trying to fix the binding on this old dictionary I have. It's a Merriam-Webster from 1989. You know, before they started adding all those ridiculous internet words. I hate how they put words like 'selfie' or 'tweet' in the dictionary now. A dictionary should have some dignity, right? My grandfather had this massive leather-bound encyclopedia set. Encyclopedia Britannica. They took up an entire mahogany bookshelf in his study. That room always smelled like pipe tobacco and old paper. He used to smoke this cherry blend—Cherry Cavendish, I think it was. You can't even smoke indoors anywhere anymore. I mean, I completely get why, the secondhand smoke and the health risks, but it really was such a comforting smell. It always reminds me of being ten years old, sitting on his rug and reading the entries about the Apollo missions. The photos of the moon landing in those old books were incredible, just that stark contrast of the white suits against the pitch-black sky... 

But... I'm sorry, what did you ask? If I try to stop? Yeah, I just don't really know. I don't think I even pause long enough to try.

I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, Doctor. Well, what brings me in is... I just feel like I’m constantly running on a motor lately. Just the other day, I was helping one of my kids, Jason, with this math worksheet, and he's got this intense fascination with trains, so we always have to make the word problems about trains to keep him engaged. Was there a specific time when it started, or has it always been pretty much the way you are?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, I've always been an active guy, but this specific level of just... not being able to stop? I haven't really noticed it coming and going like that. It just feels like it's always on. Every single day, from the minute I wake up. Our neighborhood has this new waste management contract, and they switched the pickup days from Tuesday to Thursday. Let me tell you, it threw the whole street off. People were leaving their bins out for three days straight. I remember when she tried to get everyone to paint their mailboxes the exact same shade of beige. \"Desert Sand,\" I think it was called. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I haven't really noticed it stopping or anything. It's just... constant. Just always going.\n\nDoctor: During these eight months, have there been times when you felt even MORE energized, more talkative, or needed less sleep than usual—like a few days or a week where things felt especially intense—followed by times when things calmed down a bit? Or has it really just been this same constant level the whole time?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. It hasn't been like a rollercoaster or anything, you know? It's just... it's just this one steady, constant hum. Just one speed the whole time. Like a treadmill that's stuck on a jog. Speaking of treadmills, I actually tried to buy one off Craigslist a few weeks ago to maybe burn off some of this energy. The guy lived out in the suburbs, right near that old mall they just tore down. It's a shame about that mall, honestly. I used to go there when I was a teenager, they had this incredible arcade. Pac-Man, Galaga, Asteroids... I could spend hours in there with just a pocket full of quarters. Quarters used to go so much further back then, didn't they? Now you can't even get a gumball for a quarter. I think they want like a dollar for those machines now. It's robbery! And the gumballs are always stale anyway. \n\nBut yeah, the treadmill... I went all the way out there, and the belt was completely warped. Just totally useless. So I didn't buy it. But no, to answer your question, it hasn't been up and down. Just the same constant motor since the uncle moved in.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing these activities—grading papers, pulling weeds, fixing things around the house—are you able to focus and concentrate on what you're doing, or do you find your mind is jumping around from one thought to another?\n\nPatient: Well, I can focus sometimes, I guess, but mostly it's... my mind is definitely jumping around. Like with the grading, I'll be looking at a paper—I teach special ed, mostly middle schoolers right now—and I'll see a kid wrote something about, say, a dog. And then I'm thinking about my neighbor's dog, the terrier mix I mentioned, and then I'm wondering if I remembered to buy dog treats for when my brother visits, because his golden retriever has this sensitive stomach. You have to get these specific grain-free salmon bites, they cost an absolute fortune. I was at the pet store last week looking for them, and they had this huge display of those little sweaters for dogs. Who puts a sweater on a golden retriever? They have a double coat! It's ridiculous. The girl working there tried to tell me it was for \"anxiety,\" like a thunder shirt, but it just looked like a cable-knit argyle pattern to me. \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, the papers. Yeah, I'll be grading, and then suddenly I'm up and checking the pantry for the salmon treats, and the paper is just sitting there. So, no, I wouldn't say I'm really focusing on the weeds or the fixing or any of it. My brain is just kind of bouncing off the walls the whole time.\n\nDoctor: When your mind is jumping around like that, bouncing from one thought to another—are those thoughts mostly worries or concerns about things that could go wrong, or are they just random thoughts and ideas that pop into your head without any particular worry attached to them?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, it's not really worries. I'm not... I'm not sitting there stressing about the mortgage or the state of the world or anything like that. It's really just totally random stuff that pops in. Like, I'll be thinking about whether I need to buy more wood glue for this chair I'm trying to fix. \n\nI was at Home Depot the other day, actually, looking for wood glue, and I realized they completely rearranged the aisles again. Why do they do that? You finally figure out where the fasteners are, and suddenly they've moved them all the way over near the garden supplies. I spent twenty minutes just looking for a specific size of wingnut. The guy helping me—older guy, name tag said Carl—he told me corporate makes them change the layout every two years just to force people to walk past different products and buy things they don't need. Seems like a terrible business model if you ask me, because I just got frustrated and left without the wingnuts. \n\nI ended up going to this little local hardware store downtown instead. You know, the one with the squeaky wooden floors? They've been there since the 70s. The owner's son runs it now, and he still uses one of those old heavy metal cash registers with the big buttons. The bell on it has this great ring to it, you know? Not like those terrible electronic beeps at the self-checkout. I can't stand the self-checkout. It always tells me there's an unexpected item in the bagging area when there isn't. \n\nBut yeah, what were we... oh. No, not worries. Just random things like that.\n\nDoctor: Has this constant motor feeling and the jumping around of your thoughts been causing you problems—like at work, in your relationships, or just making you feel distressed? Or does it feel more like just the way you operate?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, it's definitely causing problems. I wish it was just the way I operate, but... at work, the complaints have actually gotten serious enough that the principal put me on a corrective plan. A corrective plan! I haven't been on one of those since my very first year of teaching. Back then, they used those carbon copy forms, you know? You had to press down really hard with a ballpoint pen to make sure the yellow and pink sheets got the ink. I always hated the pink sheets. They smelled weird, like chemicals. Actually, all paper smelled different back then. Mimeograph machines! Do you remember those? The purple ink? I used to love handing out those worksheets, the kids would all sniff the paper before they started their math. You can't do that with laser printers. It's just hot toner. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, the corrective plan. And at home, it's not much better. My spouse says the caregiving routines for my uncle are just so much harder now because everyone has to manage *my* energy on top of everything else. Which isn't fair to her. She's already doing so much, organizing his pills and everything. Those daily pill organizers are so flimsy, by the way. The little plastic lids snap off after like two weeks. I tried supergluing the 'Thursday' lid back on last week, but the glue got stuck to my thumb, and I spent an hour trying to peel it off with nail polish remover. Acetone is terrible for your skin, just dries it right out. I had to use this heavy-duty lotion my wife keeps by the kitchen sink, the one with the oatmeal in it. \n\nWhat was I... oh. The distress, right. Yeah, it is distressing. I feel like I’m damaging trust with my colleagues and my family. And the worst part is, I’m really just trying to help! You really have to be careful with food analogies nowadays. When I was in middle school, teachers just brought in whatever they wanted. I remember my science teacher, Mr. Henderson, bringing in a whole sheet cake once just to show us tectonic plates. \n\nBut anyway, I finish the quizzes, and instantly I'm up and deciding I need to fix the screen on the back porch. Well, that's kind of why I was saying earlier that this is really the main thing that brought me in. It's completely automatic. Like I said, I feel like I’m just constantly running on a motor lately. I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, Doctor. Well, what brings me in is... I just feel like I’m constantly running on a motor lately. Like, I finish one thing at school—I teach special education, by the way. Middle school. Which is chaotic enough, but I really do love it. Just the other day, I was helping one of my kids, Jason, with this math worksheet, and he's got this intense fascination with trains, so we always have to make the word problems about trains to keep him engaged. I actually took a train down to Chicago last summer, the Amtrak, and the dining car was completely out of coffee by 9 AM. Can you believe that? Who runs a train and runs out of coffee that early? The conductor looked so stressed out. \n\nBut anyway, what I mean is, I finish one thing at school, or even at home, and I just immediately jump right into the next thing without ever being able to shut myself off. Like I'll get home and start grading papers, and then suddenly I'm out in the yard pulling weeds, and then I'm fixing a hinge on the back door. I just can't stop the motor, you know? It's just... go, go, go.\n\nDoctor: When did you first start noticing this 'running on a motor' feeling? Was there a specific time when it started, or has it always been pretty much the way you are?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, I've always been an active guy, but this specific level of just... not being able to stop? That really started about eight months ago. That's when our older relative moved in with us. My wife's uncle, actually. We had to completely clear out the downstairs guest room for him, which used to be my office—well, more like a storage room if I'm being honest. \n\nWhile I was clearing it out, I actually found this huge box of my old vinyl records! I spent half the night trying to hook up my old turntable, but the drive belt was completely rotted out. You know how rubber gets when it just sits in a dry room for twenty years? It just crumbles right in your hands like dust. I had to order a replacement part from this little specialty audio shop all the way out in Ohio. The guy on the phone there was great, though, he knew exactly what model I was talking about just from the description of the tonearm. \n\nBut, wait, what was I... oh, right. Yeah, the motor feeling. It's been about eight months. Ever since he moved in, it's just been dialed up to an eleven.\n\nDoctor: Over these past eight months, has this 'running on a motor' feeling been pretty much constant every day, or has it come and gone in distinct periods—like times when it's really intense and other times when it calms down?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess... I haven't really noticed it coming and going like that. It just feels like it's always on. Every single day, from the minute I wake up. Which is usually around 5 AM, because the neighbor's dog—this little terrier mix, cute thing, but man, does he have a set of lungs on him. He starts barking at the garbage trucks. Our neighborhood has this new waste management contract, and they switched the pickup days from Tuesday to Thursday. Let me tell you, it threw the whole street off. People were leaving their bins out for three days straight. The HOA president, Susan, she sent out this passive-aggressive email about \"curb appeal\" and \"community standards.\" She's always on about the community standards. I remember when she tried to get everyone to paint their mailboxes the exact same shade of beige. \"Desert Sand,\" I think it was called. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I haven't really noticed it stopping or anything. It's just... constant. Just always going.\n\nDoctor: During these eight months, have there been times when you felt even MORE energized, more talkative, or needed less sleep than usual—like a few days or a week where things felt especially intense—followed by times when things calmed down a bit? Or has it really just been this same constant level the whole time?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. It hasn't been like a rollercoaster or anything, you know? It's just... it's just this one steady, constant hum. Just one speed the whole time. Like a treadmill that's stuck on a jog. Speaking of treadmills, I actually tried to buy one off Craigslist a few weeks ago to maybe burn off some of this energy. The guy lived out in the suburbs, right near that old mall they just tore down. It's a shame about that mall, honestly. I used to go there when I was a teenager, they had this incredible arcade. Pac-Man, Galaga, Asteroids... I could spend hours in there with just a pocket full of quarters. Quarters used to go so much further back then, didn't they? Now you can't even get a gumball for a quarter. I think they want like a dollar for those machines now. It's robbery! And the gumballs are always stale anyway. \n\nBut yeah, the treadmill... I went all the way out there, and the belt was completely warped. Just totally useless. So I didn't buy it. But no, to answer your question, it hasn't been up and down. Just the same constant motor since the uncle moved in.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing these activities—grading papers, pulling weeds, fixing things around the house—are you able to focus and concentrate on what you're doing, or do you find your mind is jumping around from one thought to another?\n\nPatient: Well, I can focus sometimes, I guess, but mostly it's... my mind is definitely jumping around. Like with the grading, I'll be looking at a paper—I teach special ed, mostly middle schoolers right now—and I'll see a kid wrote something about, say, a dog. And then I'm thinking about my neighbor's dog, the terrier mix I mentioned, and then I'm wondering if I remembered to buy dog treats for when my brother visits, because his golden retriever has this sensitive stomach. You have to get these specific grain-free salmon bites, they cost an absolute fortune. I was at the pet store last week looking for them, and they had this huge display of those little sweaters for dogs. Who puts a sweater on a golden retriever? They have a double coat! It's ridiculous. The girl working there tried to tell me it was for \"anxiety,\" like a thunder shirt, but it just looked like a cable-knit argyle pattern to me. \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right, the papers. Yeah, I'll be grading, and then suddenly I'm up and checking the pantry for the salmon treats, and the paper is just sitting there. So, no, I wouldn't say I'm really focusing on the weeds or the fixing or any of it. My brain is just kind of bouncing off the walls the whole time.\n\nDoctor: When your mind is jumping around like that, bouncing from one thought to another—are those thoughts mostly worries or concerns about things that could go wrong, or are they just random thoughts and ideas that pop into your head without any particular worry attached to them?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, it's not really worries. I'm not... I'm not sitting there stressing about the mortgage or the state of the world or anything like that. It's really just totally random stuff that pops in. Like, I'll be thinking about whether I need to buy more wood glue for this chair I'm trying to fix. \n\nI was at Home Depot the other day, actually, looking for wood glue, and I realized they completely rearranged the aisles again. Why do they do that? You finally figure out where the fasteners are, and suddenly they've moved them all the way over near the garden supplies. I spent twenty minutes just looking for a specific size of wingnut. The guy helping me—older guy, name tag said Carl—he told me corporate makes them change the layout every two years just to force people to walk past different products and buy things they don't need. Seems like a terrible business model if you ask me, because I just got frustrated and left without the wingnuts. \n\nI ended up going to this little local hardware store downtown instead. You know, the one with the squeaky wooden floors? They've been there since the 70s. The owner's son runs it now, and he still uses one of those old heavy metal cash registers with the big buttons. The bell on it has this great ring to it, you know? Not like those terrible electronic beeps at the self-checkout. I can't stand the self-checkout. It always tells me there's an unexpected item in the bagging area when there isn't. \n\nBut yeah, what were we... oh. No, not worries. Just random things like that.\n\nDoctor: Has this constant motor feeling and the jumping around of your thoughts been causing you problems—like at work, in your relationships, or just making you feel distressed? Or does it feel more like just the way you operate?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, it's definitely causing problems. I wish it was just the way I operate, but... at work, the complaints have actually gotten serious enough that the principal put me on a corrective plan. A corrective plan! I haven't been on one of those since my very first year of teaching. Back then, they used those carbon copy forms, you know? You had to press down really hard with a ballpoint pen to make sure the yellow and pink sheets got the ink. I always hated the pink sheets. They smelled weird, like chemicals. Actually, all paper smelled different back then. Mimeograph machines! Do you remember those? The purple ink? I used to love handing out those worksheets, the kids would all sniff the paper before they started their math. You can't do that with laser printers. It's just hot toner. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, the corrective plan. And at home, it's not much better. My spouse says the caregiving routines for my uncle are just so much harder now because everyone has to manage *my* energy on top of everything else. Which isn't fair to her. She's already doing so much, organizing his pills and everything. Those daily pill organizers are so flimsy, by the way. The little plastic lids snap off after like two weeks. I tried supergluing the 'Thursday' lid back on last week, but the glue got stuck to my thumb, and I spent an hour trying to peel it off with nail polish remover. Acetone is terrible for your skin, just dries it right out. I had to use this heavy-duty lotion my wife keeps by the kitchen sink, the one with the oatmeal in it. \n\nWhat was I... oh. The distress, right. Yeah, it is distressing. I feel like I’m damaging trust with my colleagues and my family. And the worst part is, I’m really just trying to help! I want to be helpful, but I guess I'm just making everything harder for everyone.\n\nDoctor: When you're trying to do something—like grading those papers or fixing something around the house—do you actually have trouble getting started on it, or trouble finishing it once you've started? Or is it more that you can do these things, but you just feel this constant urge to move on to the next thing?\n\nPatient: Well, getting started isn't really the problem. It's exactly what you said at the end there. Actually, that's really what brings me in here in the first place. I just feel like I’m constantly running on a motor lately. Like I finish one thing at school or at home, and I immediately jump right into the next without ever being able to shut myself off. \n\nLike yesterday, I finished grading a batch of math quizzes—we're doing fractions right now. I tried using pizza slices on the board to explain common denominators this year, but one of my kids is lactose intolerant and got really distracted talking about dairy allergies, which I completely didn't see coming. You really have to be careful with food analogies nowadays. When I was in middle school, teachers just brought in whatever they wanted. I remember my science teacher, Mr. Henderson, bringing in a whole sheet cake once just to show us tectonic plates. \n\nBut anyway, I finish the quizzes, and instantly I'm up and deciding I need to fix the screen on the back porch. But to do that, you need one of those little spline rollers. So I go out to the garage to find the roller, and I see the lawnmower, and I realize I never changed the spark plug. Have you ever tried to find a replacement spark plug for a fifteen-year-old Toro mower? It's practically impossible. I went down this huge internet rabbit hole about small engine repair. There's this guy on YouTube who does these amazing tutorials from his shed in Minnesota. He has this massive beard, kind of like ZZ Top, and he always starts his videos by drinking coffee out of this giant plaid thermos. I used to have a thermos just like it, actually, the old Stanley ones that weigh about ten pounds. They don't make them like that anymore, the new ones dent if you just look at them wrong. \n\nBut... wait, what was I saying? Oh. Getting started. No, I start things just fine. It's just that motor feeling. I just jump to the next thing and the next thing and I can't shut it off.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing something—like grading papers or working on a task—and you get the urge to move to the next thing, do you actually try to stop yourself and finish what you're doing, or do you find that you just can't help but switch over to the next activity even when you want to stay focused?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I haven't really noticed if I'm actively trying to stop myself or not. I don't think I even realize I'm doing it until I'm already halfway through the next thing, you know? Like... there's no debate in my head about staying focused. It's just, boom, I'm doing something else. \n\nLike the other night, I was trying to fix the binding on this old dictionary I have. It's a Merriam-Webster from 1989. You know, before they started adding all those ridiculous internet words. I hate how they put words like 'selfie' or 'tweet' in the dictionary now. A dictionary should have some dignity, right? My grandfather had this massive leather-bound encyclopedia set. Encyclopedia Britannica. They took up an entire mahogany bookshelf in his study. That room always smelled like pipe tobacco and old paper. He used to smoke this cherry blend—Cherry Cavendish, I think it was. You can't even smoke indoors anywhere anymore. I mean, I completely get why, the secondhand smoke and the health risks, but it really was such a comforting smell. It always reminds me of being ten years old, sitting on his rug and reading the entries about the Apollo missions. The photos of the moon landing in those old books were incredible, just that stark contrast of the white suits against the pitch-black sky... \n\nBut... I'm sorry, what did you ask? If I try to stop? Yeah, I just don't really know. I don't think I even pause long enough to try.\n\nDoctor: When you get that urge to jump to the next thing, does it feel like something you could resist if you really tried hard—like you have the ability to stop yourself but choose not to—or does it feel completely automatic, like you literally can't hold back the impulse even if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: Well, that's kind of why I was saying earlier that this is really the main thing that brought me in. It's completely automatic. Like I said, I feel like I’m just constantly running on a motor lately. I finish one thing at school, or at home, and I just immediately jump right into the next without ever being able to shut myself off. I literally can't shut it off. \n\nSpeaking of shutting off, the heating system in my classroom is exactly like that. The radiator just clanks and hisses all winter long, and the valve is completely stripped so you can't turn it down. The district maintenance guy, Frank—he's been there forever, he actually wears these vintage denim overalls to work every day. You don't see people wearing overalls much anymore unless it's a fashion statement, but Frank actually wears them for the pockets. He keeps all his wrenches in there. He told me he buys them from this specific farm supply store in the next county over because the denim is thicker. I think it's a local place, not one of those big chains. Anyway, they used to sell baby chicks there in the spring. When my kids were little, we used to go just to look at the chicks under those big red heat lamps. They make that constant little peeping noise, hundreds of them all at once. It's deafening if you stand there long enough... \n\nBut, um... right, the impulse. No, I definitely don't choose to do it. It's just like that motor. I can't shut myself off, I just automatically jump to the next thing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, no, it definitely didn't just start recently. It's been forever. My mother used to joke that even back in elementary school, I basically taught the class from my seat. 

We had those old wooden desks back then, you know? The ones where the whole top lifts up on hinges, and they had that little groove at the top for your pencil. I used to keep a huge collection of Matchbox cars in mine. The original die-cast ones, they were so heavy compared to the cheap plastic ones they make now. My favorite was this metallic green Mustang. I actually saw one just like it at an antique mall a few weeks ago. Antique malls are fascinating, aren't they? You go in looking for one thing and end up looking at old rotary phones and Pyrex bowls for three hours. My wife loves that vintage Pyrex, the ones with the little yellow daisies on the side. We have a whole cabinet full of them now, though you really aren't supposed to put them in the dishwasher because the detergent fades the paint...

But... wait, what was I talking about? Oh, right, my mom. Yeah, she joked I taught the class because I was just always out of my chair. I'd be talking constantly, or cutting in before the teacher even finished giving the directions. So, yeah, this was part of who I was long before adulthood. It's definitely not something that just started with this family change.

I remember my science teacher, Mr. Henderson, bringing in a whole sheet cake once just to show us tectonic plates. \n\nBut anyway, I finish the quizzes, and instantly I'm up and deciding I need to fix the screen on the back porch. But to do that, you need one of those little spline rollers. So I go out to the garage to find the roller, and I see the lawnmower, and I realize I never changed the spark plug. Have you ever tried to find a replacement spark plug for a fifteen-year-old Toro mower? It's practically impossible. I went down this huge internet rabbit hole about small engine repair. There's this guy on YouTube who does these amazing tutorials from his shed in Minnesota. He has this massive beard, kind of like ZZ Top, and he always starts his videos by drinking coffee out of this giant plaid thermos. I used to have a thermos just like it, actually, the old Stanley ones that weigh about ten pounds. They don't make them like that anymore, the new ones dent if you just look at them wrong. \n\nBut... wait, what was I saying? Oh. Getting started. No, I start things just fine. It's just that motor feeling. I just jump to the next thing and the next thing and I can't shut it off.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing something—like grading papers or working on a task—and you get the urge to move to the next thing, do you actually try to stop yourself and finish what you're doing, or do you find that you just can't help but switch over to the next activity even when you want to stay focused?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, to be honest. I haven't really noticed if I'm actively trying to stop myself or not. I don't think I even realize I'm doing it until I'm already halfway through the next thing, you know? Like... there's no debate in my head about staying focused. It's just, boom, I'm doing something else. \n\nLike the other night, I was trying to fix the binding on this old dictionary I have. It's a Merriam-Webster from 1989. You know, before they started adding all those ridiculous internet words. I hate how they put words like 'selfie' or 'tweet' in the dictionary now. A dictionary should have some dignity, right? My grandfather had this massive leather-bound encyclopedia set. Encyclopedia Britannica. They took up an entire mahogany bookshelf in his study. That room always smelled like pipe tobacco and old paper. He used to smoke this cherry blend—Cherry Cavendish, I think it was. You can't even smoke indoors anywhere anymore. I mean, I completely get why, the secondhand smoke and the health risks, but it really was such a comforting smell. It always reminds me of being ten years old, sitting on his rug and reading the entries about the Apollo missions. The photos of the moon landing in those old books were incredible, just that stark contrast of the white suits against the pitch-black sky... \n\nBut... I'm sorry, what did you ask? If I try to stop? Yeah, I just don't really know. I don't think I even pause long enough to try.\n\nDoctor: When you get that urge to jump to the next thing, does it feel like something you could resist if you really tried hard—like you have the ability to stop yourself but choose not to—or does it feel completely automatic, like you literally can't hold back the impulse even if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: Well, that's kind of why I was saying earlier that this is really the main thing that brought me in. It's completely automatic. Like I said, I feel like I’m just constantly running on a motor lately. I finish one thing at school, or at home, and I just immediately jump right into the next without ever being able to shut myself off. I literally can't shut it off. \n\nSpeaking of shutting off, the heating system in my classroom is exactly like that. The radiator just clanks and hisses all winter long, and the valve is completely stripped so you can't turn it down. The district maintenance guy, Frank—he's been there forever, he actually wears these vintage denim overalls to work every day. You don't see people wearing overalls much anymore unless it's a fashion statement, but Frank actually wears them for the pockets. He keeps all his wrenches in there. He told me he buys them from this specific farm supply store in the next county over because the denim is thicker. I think it's a local place, not one of those big chains. Anyway, they used to sell baby chicks there in the spring. When my kids were little, we used to go just to look at the chicks under those big red heat lamps. They make that constant little peeping noise, hundreds of them all at once. It's deafening if you stand there long enough... \n\nBut, um... right, the impulse. No, I definitely don't choose to do it. It's just like that motor. I can't shut myself off, I just automatically jump to the next thing.\n\nDoctor: Going back to when you were a kid—say, in elementary school or middle school—did you have this same kind of 'running on a motor' feeling and jumping from one thing to another, or is this something that's really only started in the last eight months?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, it definitely didn't just start recently. It's been forever. My mother used to joke that even back in elementary school, I basically taught the class from my seat. \n\nWe had those old wooden desks back then, you know? The ones where the whole top lifts up on hinges, and they had that little groove at the top for your pencil. I used to keep a huge collection of Matchbox cars in mine. The original die-cast ones, they were so heavy compared to the cheap plastic ones they make now. My favorite was this metallic green Mustang. I actually saw one just like it at an antique mall a few weeks ago. Antique malls are fascinating, aren't they? You go in looking for one thing and end up looking at old rotary phones and Pyrex bowls for three hours. My wife loves that vintage Pyrex, the ones with the little yellow daisies on the side. We have a whole cabinet full of them now, though you really aren't supposed to put them in the dishwasher because the detergent fades the paint...\n\nBut... wait, what was I talking about? Oh, right, my mom. Yeah, she joked I taught the class because I was just always out of my chair. I'd be talking constantly, or cutting in before the teacher even finished giving the directions. So, yeah, this was part of who I was long before adulthood. It's definitely not something that just started with this family change.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

It's completely automatic. Like I said, I feel like I’m just constantly running on a motor lately. He told me he buys them from this specific farm supply store in the next county over because the denim is thicker. I think it's a local place, not one of those big chains. Anyway, they used to sell baby chicks there in the spring. When my kids were little, we used to go just to look at the chicks under those big red heat lamps. It's just like that motor. I can't shut myself off, I just automatically jump to the next thing.\n\nDoctor: Going back to when you were a kid—say, in elementary school or middle school—did you have this same kind of 'running on a motor' feeling and jumping from one thing to another, or is this something that's really only started in the last eight months?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, it definitely didn't just start recently. It's been forever. My mother used to joke that even back in elementary school, I basically taught the class from my seat. \n\nWe had those old wooden desks back then, you know? The ones where the whole top lifts up on hinges, and they had that little groove at the top for your pencil. I used to keep a huge collection of Matchbox cars in mine. The original die-cast ones, they were so heavy compared to the cheap plastic ones they make now. My favorite was this metallic green Mustang. I actually saw one just like it at an antique mall a few weeks ago. Antique malls are fascinating, aren't they? So, yeah, this was part of who I was long before adulthood. Well, you know, I'm not entirely sure if it caused real problems back then. I mean, they definitely complained, I remember that much. The teachers would always write those little notes on the report cards—'talks too much,' 'needs to stay in his seat.' But I don't remember if it actually tanked my grades or anything. 

Report cards used to be so different, didn't they? They were on that thick cardstock, and the teacher had to handwrite everything. My third-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable, she had the most incredible penmanship. Perfect cursive. They don't even teach cursive in schools anymore, which is just wild to me. How are kids supposed to sign their names on a check or a legal document? I guess nobody writes checks anymore either. I still do, at the grocery store sometimes. The cashiers always look at me like I just handed them an artifact from ancient Egypt. It's funny, though, because writing a check actually forces you to keep track of your money better. My dad used to sit at the kitchen table every Sunday morning with his checkbook and this big gray calculator with the paper roll. You could hear that thing clicking and whirring from the living room. He drank his coffee black out of this ugly brown mug that he got at a hardware store grand opening...

But, um... wait, what were you asking? Oh, if I got in trouble. I mean, probably? I was in the principal's office a few times, but I don't really remember it being a huge disaster. I think they just kind of accepted that I was a handful.

I can't shut myself off, I just automatically jump to the next thing.\n\nDoctor: Going back to when you were a kid—say, in elementary school or middle school—did you have this same kind of 'running on a motor' feeling and jumping from one thing to another, or is this something that's really only started in the last eight months?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, it definitely didn't just start recently. I actually saw one just like it at an antique mall a few weeks ago. Antique malls are fascinating, aren't they? You go in looking for one thing and end up looking at old rotary phones and Pyrex bowls for three hours. My wife loves that vintage Pyrex, the ones with the little yellow daisies on the side. We have a whole cabinet full of them now, though you really aren't supposed to put them in the dishwasher because the detergent fades the paint...\n\nBut... wait, what was I talking about? Oh, right, my mom. Yeah, she joked I taught the class because I was just always out of my chair. I'd be talking constantly, or cutting in before the teacher even finished giving the directions. So, yeah, this was part of who I was long before adulthood. It's definitely not something that just started with this family change.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school and your mom noticed you were always out of your seat and talking—did that cause you real problems? Like, were you getting in trouble at school, struggling to keep up with your grades, or did teachers complain to your parents about it?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, I'm not entirely sure if it caused real problems back then. I mean, they definitely complained, I remember that much. The teachers would always write those little notes on the report cards—'talks too much,' 'needs to stay in his seat.' But I don't remember if it actually tanked my grades or anything. \n\nReport cards used to be so different, didn't they? They were on that thick cardstock, and the teacher had to handwrite everything. My third-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable, she had the most incredible penmanship. Perfect cursive. They don't even teach cursive in schools anymore, which is just wild to me. How are kids supposed to sign their names on a check or a legal document? I guess nobody writes checks anymore either. I still do, at the grocery store sometimes. The cashiers always look at me like I just handed them an artifact from ancient Egypt. It's funny, though, because writing a check actually forces you to keep track of your money better. My dad used to sit at the kitchen table every Sunday morning with his checkbook and this big gray calculator with the paper roll. You could hear that thing clicking and whirring from the living room. He drank his coffee black out of this ugly brown mug that he got at a hardware store grand opening...\n\nBut, um... wait, what were you asking? Oh, if I got in trouble. I mean, probably? I was in the principal's office a few times, but I don't really remember it being a huge disaster. I think they just kind of accepted that I was a handful.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Well, you know, I really don't remember if I fell behind in reading or math. I mean, I guess I must have kept up okay because I ended up going to college and becoming a teacher myself. 

Although, math was always a bit of a struggle, but I think that was just because of the way they taught it back then. We had these huge textbooks with the answers to the odd-numbered questions in the back. Do you remember those? We used to try and peel the cover off the spine just enough to see if they hid the even answers in there too. Spoiler alert, they didn't. They were bound so tightly, those books. Nothing like the flimsy paperbacks they give kids now. My students tear through them in a month. I'm constantly taping them back together. I buy this special book-binding tape online, it's clear but it has these little threads running through it for strength. It's expensive, but it works so much better than regular packing tape. Packing tape just gets yellow and peels off after a year. I used it once on a box I put in the attic, full of old vinyl records. When I went up there last summer, the tape had basically turned to dust. The records were fine, though. Mostly old jazz stuff my uncle gave me. He played the trumpet, actually. Played in a band that toured the Midwest in the seventies...

But... I'm sorry, what was the question? Oh, falling behind. Yeah, I don't really know. I don't think it was a big deal academically. I just couldn't sit still.

It's funny, though, because writing a check actually forces you to keep track of your money better. My dad used to sit at the kitchen table every Sunday morning with his checkbook and this big gray calculator with the paper roll. You could hear that thing clicking and whirring from the living room. He drank his coffee black out of this ugly brown mug that he got at a hardware store grand opening...\n\nBut, um... wait, what were you asking? Oh, if I got in trouble. I mean, probably?
